## SPECIAL ISSUE

Connecting hydrological modelling and forecasting from global to local scales: Perspectives from an international joint virtual workshop [SOURCE: Page_1]

Correspondence Antara Dasgupta, Remote Sensing                   Abstract Working Group, Institute of Informatics,          The unprecedented progress in ensemble hydro-meteorological modelling and University of Osnabrueck, Osnabrück, forecasting on a range of temporal and spatial scales, raises a variety of new Germany. Email: antara.dasgupta@uni-                       challenges which formed the theme of the Joint Virtual Workshop, ‘Connect- osnabrueck.de                                     ing global to local hydrological modelling and forecasting: challenges and scientific advances’. Held from 29 June to 1 July 2021, this workshop was co- Funding information Engineering and Physical Sciences                 organised by the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts Research Council, Grant/Award Number:             (ECMWF), the Copernicus Emergency Management (CEMS) and Climate EP/R513301/1; H2020 Societal Challenges, Grant/Award Numbers: 101003876, Change (C3S) Services, the Hydrological Ensemble Prediction EXperiment 101037293; Natural Environment                    (HEPEX), and the Global Flood Partnership (GFP). This article aims to sum- Research Council, Grant/Award Number:             marise the state-of-the-art presented at the workshop and provide an early NE/S015590/1 career perspective. Recent advances in hydrological modelling and forecasting, reflections on the use of forecasts for decision-making across scales, and means to minimise new barriers to communication in the virtual format are also dis- cussed. Thematic foci of the workshop included hydrological model develop- ment and skill assessment, uncertainty communication, forecasts for early action, co-production of services and incorporation of local knowledge, Earth observation, and data assimilation. Connecting hydrological services to societal needs and local decision-making through effective communication, capacity- building and co-production was identified as critical. Multidisciplinary collabo- rations emerged as crucial to effectively bring newly developed tools to practice. [SOURCE: Page_2]

KEYWORDS communication, co-production, earth observation, earth system, forecasting, hydrological modelling, hydrological services, uncertainty [SOURCE: Page_2]

F I G U R E 2 Layout of ECMWF's Gather.Town environment for the workshop. Participants joined the sessions to view posters, attend activities, and meet their peers to foster discussions on global hydrological forecasting and how it can be better linked to local scale needs [SOURCE: Page_4]

following sections provide a review of the work presented under each of the five key themes (acknowledging that, in many cases, one presentation may contribute to multi- ple themes). The presentations provide an overview of the challenges and advances in global hydrological modelling and forecasting, and of the research and appli- cations that endeavour to effectively connect these global efforts to local scale decision-making. The sixth theme, ‘Earth System Modelling’, is woven throughout the five subsections. The final section of this article concludes with a discussion focussing around two questions: [SOURCE: Page_4]

1. How effective was the digital format in representing a broad view and bringing a global audience together? 2. In which direction is the field of global to local hydro- logical forecasting moving as a whole? [SOURCE: Page_4]

Note: Abstracts have been given a citation code based on the initials of the first author, and are listed alphabetically according to their first initial (and therefore citation code). -P indicates the abstract was presented as a lightning talk and poster, -K indicates the abstract was a keynote talk. Numbers are introduced to distinguish between topics when an author presented more than one poster, or in instances of multiple authors with the same initials. The theme(s) that each abstract relates to is also noted and indicates the section(s) under which the abstract is discussed in this article. Type of uncertainty             Description                               Models                 Workshop contributions Aleatory                        Uncertainty with stationary statistical   Single model characteristics. It may be structured    Single model + PP      DM3-P (bias, autocorrelation, long-term Multi-model persistence), but can be reduced to a stationary random distribution Epistemic (system               Uncertainty arising from a lack of        Single model           TS-P, FF-P2 dynamics)                       knowledge about how to represent         Single model +         PD-P, TJ-P, DC-P, HT-P, CPH-P, the catchment system in terms of           PP/+ML both model structure and Multi-model            BN-P, LS-P, GU-P, LC-P (deterministic parameters + probabilistic), JO-P, AW-P, PZ-P (deterministic w/PP + probabilistic), FW-P, LA-P, FJ-P, GM-P Epistemic (forcing and          Uncertainty arising from lack of          Single model           TS-P, FF-P2, response data)                  knowledge about the forcing data or      Single model + PP      PD-P, TJ-P, DC-P, DH-P, the response data with which model Multi-model            BN-P, GU-P, JO-P, LN-P, FW-P, LA-P, outputs can be evaluated. This may GM-P be because of proportionality or interpolation issues when not enough information is provided by the observational techniques to adequately describe variables required in the modelling process Epistemic (disinformation)      Uncertainties in either system            Single model           TS-P, representation or forcing data that      Single model + PP      HT-P, are known to be inconsistent or Multi-model            JO-P wrong. Semantic/ linguistic            Uncertainty about what statements or                             HT-P quantities in the relevant domain actually mean depending on the contexts or scale (e.g., storm runoff, baseflow, hydraulic conductivity, stationarity, etc.) Ontological                     Uncertainty associated with different belief systems, including what are considered the appropriate assumptions [SOURCE: Page_44]
